Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to decompose a number `num` into a form `[[k1, k2, k3, ...], r]` such that:
1. Each `ki` is greater than 1
2. The `ki` values are maximized in order (first maximizing for 2, then 3, then 4, etc.)
3. The equation holds: `2^k1 + 3^k2 + 4^k3 + ... + n^kn + r = num`

## Key Insights

1. We want to maximize the exponents starting from 2 (the smallest base) to larger bases, so we start with base 2 and try to use the largest possible exponent for base 2, then base 3, etc.

2. For each base `b`, we need to find the maximum exponent `k` such that `b^k` doesn't exceed the remaining value.

3. Since we want to maximize the exponents in order of bases (2, 3, 4, ...), we should process bases from 2 upward and for each base, use the largest possible exponent.

4. After assigning exponents to all bases up to some point, the remainder will be what's left.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Start with the input number `num`
2. Initialize an empty list for the exponents
3. For each base `b` starting from 2:
   - Find the maximum exponent `k` such that `b^k <= remaining_value`
   - If `k > 1`, add `k` to our exponents list
   - Subtract `b^k` from the remaining value
   - Continue to next base
4. The final remaining value is the remainder `r`

## Important Considerations

- We need to be careful about the order: we maximize from base 2 upward
- For larger bases, the powers grow very quickly, so we won't need very large exponents
- We stop when the remaining value is less than the base (since then no exponent > 1 can be used)

## Implementation

```python
import sys

def decompose_number(num):
    if num == 0:
        return ([], 0)
    
    exponents = []
    remaining = num
    
    # Start with base 2 and go upward
    base = 2
    while base <= remaining:
        # Find maximum exponent for current base
        power = 1
        exponent = 0
        
        # Calculate the maximum possible exponent
        temp_val = 1
        while True:
            temp_val *= base
            if temp_val > remaining:
                break
            exponent += 1
            if exponent >= 1:  # We only care about exponents > 1
                power = temp_val
        
        # If we can use at least one exponent > 1 for this base
        if exponent >= 1:
            # Use the maximum possible exponent
            power = base ** (exponent)
            remaining -= power
            exponents.append(exponent)
        else:
            # No exponent > 1 possible for this base
            break
        
        base += 1
    
    # If there's still a remainder, that's our r
    return (exponents, remaining)

# Read input
num = int(input().strip())

# Decompose the number
result = decompose_number(num)

# Output the result
print(f"[{result[0]}, {result[1]}]")
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with my logic. Let me reconsider.

The problem says we maximize for 2 then 3 then 4... meaning we want to use as many high exponents as possible starting from base 2. But in my current approach, I'm trying to find the maximum exponent for each base, which might not give the optimal decomposition.

Actually, let me think about this differently. Since we want to maximize the exponents in order (first for base 2, then base 3, etc.), we should:

1. For base 2, use the maximum possible exponent k such that 2^k <= remaining
2. For base 3, use the maximum possible exponent k such that 3^k <= remaining (after subtracting 2^k terms)
3. And so on...

But actually, looking at the examples, it seems like we're decomposing the entire number using various powers, and the remainder is what's left.

Let me correct my approach:

```python
def decompose_number(num):
    if num == 0:
        return ([], 0)
    
    exponents = []
    remaining = num
    
    base = 2
    while base <= remaining:
        # Find maximum exponent for current base
        max_exponent = 0
        power = 1
        
        # Calculate maximum exponent such that base^exponent <= remaining
        temp_val = 1
        exponent = 0
        while True:
            temp_val *= base
            if temp_val > remaining:
                break
            exponent += 1
            if exponent >= 1:  # Only consider exponents > 1
                max_exponent = exponent
        
        if max_exponent > 0:
            # Use the maximum exponent for this base
            power_val = base ** max_exponent
            remaining -= power_val
            exponents.append(max_exponent)
        else:
            # No exponent > 1 possible for this base
            break
            
        base += 1
    
    return (exponents, remaining)

# Read input
num = int(input().strip())

# Decompose the number
result = decompose_number(num)

# Output the result
print(f"[{result[0]}, {result[1]}]")

## Problem Understanding

We need to decompose a number `num` into the form `[[k1, k2, k3, ...], r]` such that:
1. Each `kn` is greater than 1
2. The `kn` values are maximized (prioritizing larger exponents first)
3. The sum of `2^k1 + 3^k2 + 4^k3 + ... + n^kn + r = num`

The key insight is that we want to maximize the exponents (starting from 2, then 3, etc.) to minimize the remainder, and we want to use the largest possible exponents first.

## Strategy

1. Start with the largest possible exponent for base 2, then move to base 3, base 4, etc.
2. For each base, try to maximize the exponent such that the sum doesn't exceed the remaining number.
3. Continue until we can't use any more terms (either the base is too large or the exponent would overshoot).
4. The remainder is what's left.

Since we want to maximize exponents, we should process from base 2 upwards, and for each base, use the largest possible exponent that doesn't make the sum exceed the current value.

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Initialize an empty list for the exponents and a variable for the remaining value.
2. For each base starting from 2:
   - Calculate the maximum possible exponent such that base^exponent ≤ remaining value
   - But also consider that we want to maximize exponents, so we want to use as many high exponents as possible
   - Actually, since we want to maximize each exponent in order (2, 3, 4, ...), we should greedily assign the maximum possible value for each base in order.

Wait, let me reconsider the examples:

In the example 26 → [[4,2],1]:
- 2^4 = 16, 3^2 = 9, 16 + 9 = 25, remainder = 1

This suggests we're assigning exponents to different bases in order.

Since the problem states "maximized (first maximizing for 2 then 3 then 4 and so on)", we should:
- For base 2, use as large an exponent as possible
- For base 3, use as large an exponent as possible given the remaining value
- And so on

But we need to be careful - we might want to skip some bases or reduce exponents to optimize the remainder.

Actually, looking at the problem, it seems we need to assign exponents to bases 2, 3, 4, ... in order, and for each base, assign the maximum possible exponent such that the sum doesn't exceed the target.

Let me implement a greedy approach:

1. Start with base 2, find the maximum exponent such that 2^exponent ≤ remaining value
2. Subtract 2^exponent from remaining value
3. Move to base 3, find maximum exponent such that 3^exponent ≤ remaining value
4. Continue until either we've processed all bases or the remaining value becomes too small

But we need to check if this gives the optimal result.

```python
def solve(num):
    if num == 0:
        return ([], 0)
    
    exponents = []
    remaining = num
    
    base = 2
    while base <= remaining:
        # Find maximum exponent for current base
        power = 1
        exp = 0
        
        # Calculate max exponent such that base^exp <= remaining
        while True:
            power *= base
            exp += 1
            if power > remaining:
                break
        
        # If we can use at least one exponent (exp >= 1), use it
        if exp > 0:
            # We can use this exponent (since exp > 0 means base^1 <= remaining)
            # But we want to maximize, so we use exp-1 (since we went one step too far)
            max_exponent = exp - 1
            
            # If using max_exponent would exceed remaining, try smaller
            if base ** max_exponent > remaining:
                max_exponent = 0
            
            # Actually, we should use the maximum possible exponent that doesn't exceed remaining
            temp_exp = 0
            temp_power = 1
            while temp_power <= remaining:
                temp_exp += 1
                temp_power *= base
                if temp_power > remaining:
                    break
            
            # temp_exp now contains the max exponent (if temp_power > remaining, then max is temp_exp-1)
            if temp_power > remaining:
                max_exponent = temp_exp - 1
            else:
                max_exponent = temp_exp
            
            # The actual max exponent we can use
            actual_max_exp = 0
            temp_power = 1
            for exp_val in range(1, 100):  # reasonable limit
                temp_power *= base
                if temp_power > remaining:
                    actual_max_exp = exp_val - 1
                    break
                actual_max_exp = exp_val
            
            # But I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me just compute it properly.
            power = 1
            exp_val = 0
            while power <= remaining:
                exp_val += 1
                power *= base
            # exp_val is now the first value where power > remaining
            # so actual max exponent is exp_val - 1
            actual_max_exp = exp_val - 1
            
            # If we can actually use this exponent (it's > 0)
            if actual_max_exp > 0:
                # Use this exponent
                exponents.append(actual_max_exp)
                remaining -= base ** actual_max_exp
            else:
                # Can't use this base (base^1 > remaining), so skip
                pass
        else:
            # Can't use this base
            pass
        
        base += 1
    
    # If there's still remaining, it's the remainder
    return (exponents, remaining)

def solve(num):

        return ([], 0)

    remaining = num

            remaining -= base ** max_exponent

            break  # Can't use this base or any higher base

```python
def solve(num):
    if num == 0:
        return ([], 0)
    
    exponents = []
    remaining = num
    
    base = 2
    while base <= remaining:
        # Find maximum exponent for current base, but exponent >= 2
        max_exponent = 0
        power = 1
        
        # Try exponents from 2 upwards
        exp_val = 2
        while True:
            power = base ** exp_val
            if power > remaining:
                break
            max_exponent = exp_val
            exp_val += 1
        
        # If we found a valid exponent (>=2) and it's not zero, use it
        if max_exponent > 0:
            exponents.append(max_exponent)
            remaining -= base ** max_exponent
        else:
            # No valid exponent ≥ 2 for this base
            break
        
        base += 1
    
    return (exponents, remaining)

```python
def solve(num):
    if num == 0:
        return ([], 0)
    
    exponents = []
    remaining = num
    
    base = 2
    while base <= remaining:
        # Find maximum exponent for current base, but exponent >= 2
        max_exponent = 0
        exp_val = 2
        
        # Try exponents from 2 upwards
        while True:
            power = base ** exp_val
            if power > remaining:
                break
            max_exponent = exp_val
            exp_val += 1
        
        # If we found a valid exponent (>=2) and it's not zero, use it
        if max_exponent > 0:
            exponents.append(max_exponent)
            remaining -= base ** max_exponent
        else:
            # No valid exponent ≥ 2 for this base
            break
        
        base += 1
    
    return (exponents, remaining)

# Read input
num = int(input().strip())

# Solve and output
result = solve(num)
print(result[0], result[1])

# Feynman's squares
Richard Phillips Feynman was a well-known American physicist and a recipient of the Nobel Prize in Physics. He worked in theoretical physics and pioneered the field of quantum computing.

Recently, an old farmer found some papers and notes that are believed to have belonged to Feynman. Among notes about mesons and electromagnetism, there was a napkin where he wrote a simple puzzle: "how many different squares are there in a grid of NxN squares?".

For example, when N=2, the answer is 5: the 2x2 square itself, plus the four 1x1 squares in its corners:



# Task

You have to write a function

```python
def count_squares(n):
```

that solves Feynman's question in general. The input to your function will always be a positive integer.

#Examples

```python
count_squares(1) =  1
count_squares(2) =  5
count_squares(3) = 14
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count how many different squares exist in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me analyze the examples:
- N=1: 1 square (the single 1×1 square)
- N=2: 5 squares (1 large 2×2 square + 4 small 1×1 squares in corners)
- N=3: 14 squares

## Pattern Recognition

For an N×N grid, we can have squares of different sizes:
- 1×1 squares
- 2×2 squares  
- 3×3 squares
- ...
- N×N squares

For each square size k×k, how many positions can we place it in an N×N grid?

To place a k×k square, we need:
- k columns of space horizontally
- k rows of space vertically

So we can position the top-left corner of the k×k square at any position where:
- Row position: 0 to (N-k)
- Column position: 0 to (N-k)

Therefore, the number of positions for k×k squares is (N-k+1) × (N-k+1) = (N-k+1)²

## Formula Derivation

Total squares = Σ(k=1 to N) of (N-k+1)²

Let me verify with the examples:

For N=2:
- k=1: (2-1+1)² = 2² = 4
- k=2: (2-2+1)² = 1² = 1
- Total: 4 + 1 = 5 ✓

For N=3:
- k=1: (3-1+1)² = 3² = 9
- k=2: (3-2+1)² = 2² = 4
- k=3: (3-3+1)² = 1² = 1
- Total: 9 + 4 + 1 = 14 ✓

Perfect! The formula works.

## Mathematical Simplification

The sum is: Σ(i=1 to N) i² where i = N-k+1

When k goes from 1 to N, i goes from N down to 1.

So we have: Σ(i=1 to N) i² = 1² + 2² + 3² + ... + N²

The sum of squares formula is: N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

Let me verify:
- N=1: 1(1+1)(2+1)/6 = 1×2×3/6 = 1 ✓
- N=2: 2(3)(5)/6 = 30/6 = 5 ✓
- N=3: 3(4)(7)/6 = 84/6 = 14 ✓

Perfect match!

## Implementation

```python
def count_squares(n):
    # Sum of squares formula: n(n+1)(2n+1)/6
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

Note: I'm using integer division (//) since the result is always an integer.

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

The problem asks for the number of different squares in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me visualize this with the examples:

- For N=1: We have just 1 square (the 1×1 square)
- For N=2: We have 5 squares total - 1 big 2×2 square + 4 small 1×1 squares in the corners

## Pattern Recognition

Let me think about what squares we can have in an N×N grid:

1. **1×1 squares**: These are the smallest squares. In an N×N grid, we can place a 1×1 square in any position where there's at least one row and one column available. We can place it in any of the N rows and any of the N columns, so there are N × N = N² of these.

2. **2×2 squares**: A 2×2 square requires 2 consecutive rows and 2 consecutive columns. The top-left corner of a 2×2 square can be placed at any position (i,j) where 1 ≤ i ≤ N-1 and 1 ≤ j ≤ N-1. So there are (N-1) × (N-1) of these.

3. **k×k squares** where k ranges from 1 to N:
   - A k×k square needs k consecutive rows and k consecutive columns
   - The top-left corner of such a square can be placed at any position (i,j) where 1 ≤ i ≤ N-k+1 and 1 ≤ j ≤ N-k+1
   - So there are (N-k+1) × (N-k+1) of these k×k squares

## General Formula

Summing up all possible squares from 1×1 to N×N:
- For k=1: (N-1+1)² = N²
- For k=2: (N-2+1)² = (N-1)²
- For k=3: (N-3+1)² = (N-2)²
- ...
- For k=N: (N-N+1)² = 1²

So total squares = Σ(k=1 to N) of (N-k+1)²

Let me change the summation index: let m = N-k+1
When k=1, m=N
When k=N, m=1

So total squares = Σ(m=1 to N) of m² = 1² + 2² + 3² + ... + N²

The formula for the sum of squares is: N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

- N=1: 1² = 1 ✓
- N=2: 1² + 2² = 1 + 4 = 5 ✓  
- N=3: 1² + 2² + 3² = 1 + 4 + 9 = 14 ✓

Perfect! This matches all the given examples.

## Solution

```python
def count_squares(n):
    # Sum of squares from 1 to n: n(n+1)(2n+1)/6
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

The formula n(n+1)(2n+1)/6 gives us the sum of squares from 1² to n², which is exactly what we need.

Let me verify once more:
- N=1: 1×2×3/6 = 6/6 = 1 ✓
- N=2: 2×3×5/6 = 30/6 = 5 ✓
- N=3: 3×4×7/6 = 84/6 = 14 ✓

Perfect, the solution is correct.

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count the total number of different squares in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me analyze the examples:
- N=1: 1×1 grid has only 1 square
- N=2: 2×2 grid has 5 squares total

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me think about what squares exist in an N×N grid:

1. **1×1 squares**: These are the individual cells. In an N×N grid, there are N × N = N² of these.

2. **2×2 squares**: These are squares made of 4 cells. To form a 2×2 square, we need a 2×2 block of cells. How many such blocks are there?
   - We can place the top-left corner of a 2×2 square in positions (1,1) through (N-1, N-1)
   - So there are (N-1) × (N-1) = (N-1)² such squares

3. **3×3 squares**: Similarly, for a k×k square, we need a k×k block of cells.
   - The top-left corner can be placed anywhere from (1,1) to (N-k+1, N-k+1)
   - So there are (N-k+1)² such squares

Generally, for a k×k square where 1 ≤ k ≤ N:
- Number of such squares = (N-k+1)²

## Total Count

Total squares = Σ(k=1 to N) (N-k+1)²

Let's rewrite this sum:
- When k=1: (N-1+1)² = N²
- When k=2: (N-2+1)² = (N-1)²  
- When k=3: (N-3+1)² = (N-2)²
- ...
- When k=N: (N-N+1)² = 1²

So total = N² + (N-1)² + (N-2)² + ... + 1²

This is the sum of squares from 1² to N².

The formula for sum of squares from 1 to N is: N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

## Verification with Examples

- N=1: 1(1+1)(2×1+1)/6 = 1×2×3/6 = 6/6 = 1 ✓
- N=2: 2(2+1)(4+1)/6 = 2×3×5/6 = 30/6 = 5 ✓  
- N=3: 3(3+1)(6+1)/6 = 3×4×7/6 = 84/6 = 14 ✓

Perfect! All examples match.

## Solution

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

The formula n(n+1)(2n+1)/6 gives the sum of squares from 1² to n², which is exactly what we need for counting all possible squares in an n×n grid.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count the total number of different squares in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me analyze the examples:
- N=1: There's only 1 square (the entire 1×1 grid)
- N=2: There are 5 squares total
- N=3: There are 14 squares total

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me think about what squares exist in an N×N grid:

1. **1×1 squares**: These are the smallest squares. In an N×N grid, there are N×N of them (one in each cell).

2. **2×2 squares**: These are squares that are 2 units by 2 units. To form a 2×2 square, we need to choose a top-left corner that can accommodate a 2×2 region. The top-left corner can be in any position from (0,0) to (N-2, N-2), so there are (N-1)×(N-1) such squares.

3. **3×3 squares**: Similarly, we can form a 3×3 square if the top-left corner is in positions from (0,0) to (N-3, N-3), giving us (N-2)×(N-2) such squares.

In general, for a k×k square where 1 ≤ k ≤ N:
- The number of such squares is (N-k+1)²

## Formula Derivation

The total number of squares is the sum of squares of (N-k+1) for k from 1 to N:

Total = Σ(k=1 to N) (N-k+1)²
= Σ(i=1 to N) i²  (where i = N-k+1)

We know that the sum of squares from 1 to N is: N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

For N=1: 1² = 1 ✓
For N=2: 1² + 2² = 1 + 4 = 5 ✓
For N=3: 1² + 2² + 3² = 1 + 4 + 9 = 14 ✓

Perfect! The pattern is confirmed.

## Final Solution

The function will calculate the sum of squares from 1 to N using the formula:
N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

Let me verify:
- N=1: 1×2×3//6 = 6//6 = 1 ✓
- N=2: 2×3×5//6 = 30//6 = 5 ✓  
- N=3: 3×4×7//6 = 84//6 = 14 ✓

The solution works correctly!

    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

The problem asks for the number of different squares in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me analyze the examples:
- N=1: 1 square (the only 1×1 square)
- N=2: 5 squares - 1 large 2×2 square + 4 small 1×1 squares
- N=3: 14 squares

## Pattern Analysis

Let me think about what squares can exist in an N×N grid:

1. **1×1 squares**: These are the smallest squares. In an N×N grid, we can place a 1×1 square at any position from (0,0) to (N-1, N-1). So there are N × N = N² such squares.

2. **2×2 squares**: These are squares of size 2×2. We can place the top-left corner at positions (0,0) to (N-2, N-2). So there are (N-1) × (N-1) = (N-1)² such squares.

3. **k×k squares**: For a k×k square, we can place its top-left corner at positions (0,0) to (N-k, N-k). So there are (N-k+1) × (N-k+1) = (N-k+1)² such squares.

## General Formula

The total number of squares is the sum of squares of all possible sizes:

- Size 1×1: (N-1+1)² = N²
- Size 2×2: (N-2+1)² = (N-1)²
- Size 3×3: (N-3+1)² = (N-2)²
- ...
- Size k×k: (N-k+1)² where k goes from 1 to N

Actually, let me reconsider the indexing. For a k×k square in an N×N grid:
- The top-left corner can be at row i where 0 ≤ i ≤ N-k
- The top-left corner can be at column j where 0 ≤ j ≤ N-k
- So the count is (N-k+1) × (N-k+1) = (N-k+1)²

Since k goes from 1 to N:
- When k=1: (N-1+1)² = N²
- When k=2: (N-2+1)² = (N-1)²
- When k=3: (N-3+1)² = (N-2)²
- ...
- When k=N: (N-N+1)² = 1² = 1

So the total count is: N² + (N-1)² + (N-2)² + ... + 1²

This is the sum of squares from 1² to N².

## Mathematical Formula

The sum of squares from 1² to N² is: N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

Let me verify with the examples:
- N=1: 1×2×3/6 = 6/6 = 1 ✓
- N=2: 2×3×5/6 = 30/6 = 5 ✓  
- N=3: 3×4×7/6 = 84/6 = 14 ✓

Perfect! The formula works.

## Implementation

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

The division by 6 is integer division since the formula always produces an integer.

    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

3. **3×3 squares**: There are (N-2)×(N-2) of these

## Problem Understanding

We need to count the number of different squares in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me visualize this with the examples:
- For N=1: We have just 1 square (the entire grid)
- For N=2: We have 1 big 2×2 square plus 4 individual 1×1 squares, totaling 5
- For N=3: We need to count all possible squares of different sizes

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **What types of squares are there?**
   - 1×1 squares
   - 2×2 squares  
   - 3×3 squares
   - And so on up to N×N squares

2. **How many of each size?**
   - For a k×k square in an N×N grid:
     - The top-left corner can be at any position where there's enough space
     - The top-left corner can be at row i (0 to N-k) and column j (0 to N-k)
     - So there are (N-k+1) × (N-k+1) possible positions for a k×k square

3. **Total count:**
   - Sum over all possible square sizes k from 1 to N
   - For each k: (N-k+1)²

So the formula is: Σ(k=1 to N) (N-k+1)² = Σ(i=1 to N) i² where i = N-k+1

When k=1: (N-1+1)² = N²
When k=2: (N-2+1)² = (N-1)²
...
When k=N: (N-N+1)² = 1²

So we need to calculate: N² + (N-1)² + (N-2)² + ... + 1²

This is the sum of squares from 1 to N, which has a known formula:
Sum = N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

Let me verify with examples:
- N=1: 1² = 1 ✓
- N=2: 1² + 2² = 1 + 4 = 5 ✓
- N=3: 1² + 2² + 3² = 1 + 4 + 9 = 14 ✓

Perfect! The formula works.

## Implementation

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

The formula N(N+1)(2N+1)/6 gives the sum of squares from 1 to N, which is exactly what we need.

Note: We use integer division (//) since the result is always an integer.

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count the total number of different squares in an N×N grid of squares.

Let me visualize this with the examples:
- For N=1: We have a 1×1 grid, so only 1 square
- For N=2: We have a 2×2 grid, which contains:
  - 1 square of size 2×2 (the entire grid)
  - 4 squares of size 1×1 (one in each corner)
  - Total: 1 + 4 = 5 squares

## Pattern Recognition

Let me think about how many squares of each size exist in an N×N grid:

1. **1×1 squares**: These are the smallest squares. In an N×N grid, we can place a 1×1 square in any of the N×N positions (since we can start at any row from 1 to N and any column from 1 to N).
   - Count = N²

2. **2×2 squares**: A 2×2 square requires 2×2 space. We can place it starting at any row from 1 to (N-1) and any column from 1 to (N-1).
   - Count = (N-1)²

3. **3×3 squares**: Requires 3×3 space. Starting positions: rows 1 to (N-2), columns 1 to (N-2)
   - Count = (N-2)²

...

k×k squares: For a k×k square to fit in an N×N grid, we need:
- Starting row: 1 to (N-k+1)
- Starting column: 1 to (N-k+1)
- So count = (N-k+1)²

The maximum k is N, when we have a single N×N square.

## Formula Derivation

The total number of squares = sum of squares from k=1 to k=N of (N-k+1)²

Let's reindex: let i = N-k+1, then when k=1, i=N; when k=N, i=1
So the sum becomes: sum from i=1 to i=N of i²

Total squares = 1² + 2² + 3² + ... + N²

The formula for sum of squares from 1 to N is: N(N+1)(2N+1)/6

Let me verify with examples:
- N=1: 1×2×3/6 = 6/6 = 1 ✓
- N=2: 2×3×5/6 = 30/6 = 5 ✓  
- N=3: 3×4×7/6 = 84/6 = 14 ✓

Perfect! The formula works.

## Solution

```python
def count_squares(n):
    return n * (n + 1) * (2 * n + 1) // 6
```

The function calculates the sum of squares from 1 to n using the mathematical formula n(n+1)(2n+1)/6, and uses integer division // since we're guaranteed to get an integer result.
